```csv
00602,Aguada,PR
00603,Aguadilla,PR
00606,Maricao,PR
00610,Anasco,PR
...
```

## Important issue
Your source ZIP column is currently showing values like:

- `601`
- `602`
- `603`

But Puerto Rico ZIPs should be:

- `00601`
- `00602`
- `00603`

So the converter **must left-pad ZIPs to 5 digits**.

That is critical.

---

## Good news
You do **not** need most of the other columns for your app:
- `lat`
- `lng`
- `state_name`
- `population`
- `county_name`
- `timezone`
- etc.

Your current ZIPKEY VB implementation only needs:

```csv
ZipCode,City,State
```

---

# Recommended output format
Exactly this:

```csv
ZipCode,City,State
00601,Adjuntas,PR
00602,Aguada,PR
00603,Aguadilla,PR
00606,Maricao,PR
00610,Anasco,PR
00611,Angeles,PR
00612,Arecibo,PR
00616,Bajadero,PR
00617,Barceloneta,PR
00622,Boqueron,PR
```

---

# Best way to convert the full file
Since the file is large, the easiest solution is a **PowerShell script**.

## Use this script
It will:
- read your downloaded CSV
- extract `zip`, `city`, `state_id`
- pad ZIPs to 5 digits
- write a clean output file in your target format

```powershell
# convert-zipcodes.ps1

$inputFile  = "C:\Temp\source-zipcodes.csv"
$outputFile = "C:\Temp\zipcodes.csv"

$rows = Import-Csv -Path $inputFile

$rows |
    Select-Object `
        @{Name='ZipCode'; Expression={ $_.zip.ToString().PadLeft(5, '0') }}, `
        @{Name='City';    Expression={ $_.city.Trim() }}, `
        @{Name='State';   Expression={ $_.state_id.Trim().ToUpper() }} |
    Export-Csv -Path $outputFile -NoTypeInformation
```

---

## One small cleanup after export
PowerShell may put quotes around values, like:

```csv
"ZipCode","City","State"
"00601","Adjuntas","PR"
```

That is still valid CSV, and your current parser may or may not like it depending on how strict it is.

### Safer option
If you want to avoid quoted output entirely, use this version instead:

```powershell
# convert-zipcodes.ps1

$inputFile  = "C:\Temp\source-zipcodes.csv"
$outputFile = "C:\Temp\zipcodes.csv"

$rows = Import-Csv -Path $inputFile

"ZipCode,City,State" | Set-Content -Path $outputFile

foreach ($row in $rows) {
    $zip = $row.zip.ToString().PadLeft(5, '0')
    $city = ($row.city -replace ',', '').Trim()
    $state = $row.state_id.Trim().ToUpper()

    "$zip,$city,$state" | Add-Content -Path $outputFile
}
```

This version:
- avoids CSV quotes
- strips commas from city names just in case
- creates exactly the format your VB parser expects now

---

## Important note about accents
Your source includes names like:
- `AÃ±asco`
- `PeÃ±uelas`

But the `city` column you pasted uses ASCII-friendly values like:
- `Anasco`
- `Penuelas`

That is actually convenient for old-system compatibility.

## @testsubject117

its just a .txt file

## Copilot

Thatâ€™s fine â€” **a `.txt` file is okay** if itâ€™s really CSV-formatted text.

## Direct answer
If the downloaded file is a text file, we just need to confirm:
- itâ€™s delimited like CSV
- and it has the header row you pasted

If so, PowerShell `Import-Csv` can still read it even if the extension is `.txt`.

---

## Use this version
Just point to the `.txt` file instead of `.csv`.

```powershell
$inputFile  = "C:\Users\CapnKirk\Downloads\simplemaps_uszips_basicv1.94\uszips.txt"
$outputFile = "\\invoice\mainmenu\data\phone\zipcodes.csv"

$rows = Import-Csv -Path $inputFile -Delimiter "`t"

"ZipCode,City,State" | Set-Content -Path $outputFile

foreach ($row in $rows) {
    $zip = $row.zip.ToString().PadLeft(5, '0')
    $city = ($row.city -replace ',', '').Trim()
    $state = $row.state_id.Trim().ToUpper()

    "$zip,$city,$state" | Add-Content -Path $outputFile
}
```

---

## Why I used `-Delimiter "`t"`
The sample you pasted looks **tab-delimited**, not comma-delimited.

Your header was shown like:

```text
zip    lat    lng    city    state_id ...
```

That suggests a **TSV** file even if it has a `.txt` extension.

So:
- `.txt` extension is fine
- tab-delimited is likely the important part

---
